\section{Foreword}\label{foreword}

IRIS is a systems and machine-learning DSL built with performance,
expressiveness, and clarity in mind. It compiles through LLVM to native
machine code, meaning the programs you write are fast --- not scripted,
not interpreted at runtime. At the same time, IRIS gives you high-level
conveniences that feel at home in a modern programming language: type
inference, closures, algebraic data types, pattern matching, channels
for concurrency, and built-in automatic differentiation.

\textbf{Who is this book for?}

This book is for programmers who have some experience in at least one
other language (Python, Rust, C, Go, or similar). You do not need to
have written a compiler or worked in ML before. The early chapters cover
fundamentals; the later chapters tackle advanced topics like tensors,
gradient descent, and native binary compilation.

\subsection{How to read this book}\label{how-to-read-this-book}

Read chapters 1 through 5 in order --- they build on each other. After
that, the chapters are largely independent. If you are specifically
interested in ML, jump to chapters 10 and 11 after chapter 5. If you
want to compile native binaries, chapter 12 stands alone.

Every chapter has working code examples you can run immediately, a ``Try
It Yourself'' section with exercises, and a ``Common Mistakes'' sidebar
covering the pitfalls that trip up new IRIS programmers.

Let's get started.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Chapter 1: Getting Started}\label{chapter-1-getting-started}

\subsection{1.1 What Is IRIS?}\label{what-is-iris}

IRIS is a statically-typed compiled language. Its pipeline looks like
this:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{.iris source file}
\NormalTok{       |}
\NormalTok{    Lexer  (text → tokens)}
\NormalTok{       |}
\NormalTok{    Parser (tokens → AST)}
\NormalTok{       |}
\NormalTok{    Lowerer (AST → IR)}
\NormalTok{       |}
\NormalTok{  Optimization passes}
\NormalTok{       |}
\NormalTok{  LLVM IR backend}
\NormalTok{       |}
\NormalTok{  clang + lld linker}
\NormalTok{       |}
\NormalTok{  Native binary (.exe on Windows)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The same source can also be run directly by the built-in tree-walking
interpreter (for quick development), or compiled all the way to a native
binary for production.

\subsection{1.2 Installation}\label{installation}

IRIS ships as a self-contained release archive that includes the
\texttt{iris} binary, the standard library, and a bundled LLVM toolchain
(clang + lld). Download the latest release for your platform from the
\href{https://github.com/paigeadelethompson/IRIS/releases}{GitHub
Releases page}.

\subsubsection{Linux}\label{linux}

\textbf{Option A --- \texttt{.deb} package (Debian / Ubuntu)}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{sudo}\NormalTok{ dpkg }\AttributeTok{{-}i}\NormalTok{ iris\_1.0.0{-}rc1\_amd64.deb   }\CommentTok{\# or arm64}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\textbf{Option B --- \texttt{.rpm} package (Fedora / RHEL / openSUSE)}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{sudo}\NormalTok{ rpm }\AttributeTok{{-}i}\NormalTok{ iris{-}1.0.0{-}rc1{-}1.x86\_64.rpm   }\CommentTok{\# or aarch64}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\textbf{Option C --- AppImage (any distro)}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{chmod}\NormalTok{ +x iris{-}1.0.0{-}rc1{-}x86\_64.AppImage}
\ExtensionTok{./iris{-}1.0.0{-}rc1{-}x86\_64.AppImage} \AttributeTok{{-}{-}version}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\textbf{Option D --- Shell installer}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ExtensionTok{curl} \AttributeTok{{-}fsSL}\NormalTok{ https://raw.githubusercontent.com/paigeadelethompson/IRIS/master/installer/linux/install.sh }\KeywordTok{|} \FunctionTok{bash}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

This installs \texttt{iris} to \texttt{\textasciitilde{}/.iris/bin} and
adds it to your \texttt{PATH}.

\subsubsection{macOS}\label{macos}

\textbf{Option A --- \texttt{.pkg} installer}

Download and double-click \texttt{iris-1.0.0-rc1-arm64.pkg} (Apple
Silicon) or \texttt{iris-1.0.0-rc1-x64.pkg} (Intel). The installer
places IRIS in \texttt{/usr/local/bin}.

\textbf{Option B --- Shell installer}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ExtensionTok{curl} \AttributeTok{{-}fsSL}\NormalTok{ https://raw.githubusercontent.com/paigeadelethompson/IRIS/master/installer/macos/install.sh }\KeywordTok{|} \FunctionTok{bash}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\textbf{Option C --- Homebrew (coming soon)}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ExtensionTok{brew}\NormalTok{ install iris{-}lang   }\CommentTok{\# planned for a future release}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Note:} On macOS, you may need to allow the binary in
\emph{System Settings → Privacy \& Security} the first time you run it.
\end{quote}

\subsubsection{Windows}\label{windows}

\textbf{Option A --- Installer (.exe)}

Download and run \texttt{iris-1.0.0-rc1-setup.exe}. The Inno Setup
installer bundles the LLVM toolchain, MinGW sysroot, and the IRIS VS
Code extension. It adds \texttt{iris} to your \texttt{PATH}
automatically.

\textbf{Option B --- Portable .zip}

Download \texttt{iris-1.0.0-rc1-windows-x64.zip}, extract it to a folder
(for example,
\texttt{C:\textbackslash{}tools\textbackslash{}iris\textbackslash{}}),
and add that folder to your \texttt{PATH}.

\textbf{Option C --- PowerShell installer}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{irm}\NormalTok{ https}\OperatorTok{://}\NormalTok{raw}\OperatorTok{.}\FunctionTok{githubusercontent}\OperatorTok{.}\FunctionTok{com}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{paigeadelethompson}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{IRIS}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{master}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{installer}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{install}\OperatorTok{.}\FunctionTok{ps1} \OperatorTok{|} \FunctionTok{iex}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{Verify the installation}\label{verify-the-installation}

Open a new terminal and run:

\begin{verbatim}
iris --version
\end{verbatim}

You should see output like:

\begin{verbatim}
iris 1.0.0-rc1 (abc1234 2026-03-02)
IRIS — Intermediate Representation for Intelligent Systems
Copyright (C) 2024-2026 Moon & IRIS Project Contributors
License: GPL-2.0-or-later <https://www.gnu.org/licenses/old-licenses/gpl-2.0.html>

Compiler:
  Version:       1.0.0-rc1
  Git commit:    abc1234567890abcdef1234567890abcdef123456
  Git branch:    main
  Build date:    2026-03-02

Platform:
  Target:        x86_64-pc-windows-msvc
  Host:          x86_64-pc-windows-msvc
  Thread model:  win32

Build:
  Profile:       release
  Opt level:     3
  Rust edition:  2021
  Built with:    rustc 1.78.0 (9b00956e5 2024-04-29)
\end{verbatim}

The version output shows the full compiler provenance: version, git
commit hash, branch, build date, platform triple, thread model,
optimization profile, and the Rust toolchain used to build it.

\subsubsection{Native compilation
dependencies}\label{native-compilation-dependencies}

For native binary compilation (\texttt{iris\ build}), IRIS requires
LLVM/clang 17+ and the \texttt{lld} linker. The official release
archives \textbf{bundle these tools automatically} --- no additional
installation is needed.

If you installed IRIS from source or want to use your own LLVM, ensure
that \texttt{clang} and \texttt{lld} are on your \texttt{PATH}:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3226}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.6774}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Platform
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
How to install LLVM
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Linux (Debian/Ubuntu) & \texttt{sudo\ apt\ install\ clang\ lld} \\
Linux (Fedora/RHEL) & \texttt{sudo\ dnf\ install\ clang\ lld} \\
macOS & \texttt{brew\ install\ llvm} (Apple's Xcode clang also works) \\
Windows & Download from \url{https://releases.llvm.org/} to
\texttt{C:\textbackslash{}Program\ Files\textbackslash{}LLVM} \\
\end{longtable}

On Windows, the linker also needs MinGW sysroot headers/libraries.
Install MSYS2 and ensure the ucrt64 files are present at
\texttt{C:\textbackslash{}msys64\textbackslash{}ucrt64}. No GCC
installation is needed --- IRIS uses clang for all compilation and lld
for linking.

\subsection{1.3 Hello, World}\label{hello-world}

Create a file named \texttt{hello.iris}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ main() }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Hello, World!"}\NormalTok{);}
    \DecValTok{0}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Run it:

\begin{verbatim}
iris run hello.iris
\end{verbatim}

Output:

\begin{verbatim}
Hello, World!
\end{verbatim}

Let's look at what each line does:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{def\ main()\ -\textgreater{}\ i64} --- defines a function
  named \texttt{main} with no parameters that returns an \texttt{i64}
  (64-bit integer).
\item
  \texttt{print("Hello,\ World!");} --- calls the built-in
  \texttt{print} function. The semicolon is required because this is a
  non-tail statement.
\item
  \texttt{0} --- the last expression in a function is its return value.
  \texttt{main} returns \texttt{0}, meaning success.
\end{itemize}

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Note:} IRIS functions return the value of their last expression.
You rarely need an explicit \texttt{return} statement. The last line
\texttt{0} is the return value --- it has no semicolon because it is the
tail expression.
\end{quote}

\subsection{1.4 The REPL}\label{the-repl}

IRIS comes with an interactive REPL (Read-Eval-Print Loop) that is great
for exploring the language:

\begin{verbatim}
iris repl
\end{verbatim}

You will see:

\begin{verbatim}
IRIS 1.0.0-rc1 REPL
  :help for commands · :quit to exit · Ctrl+D to exit

>>
\end{verbatim}

Try typing expressions:

\begin{verbatim}
>> 1 + 2
3
>> "hello"
hello
>> 42 * 10
420
\end{verbatim}

You can define functions and use them immediately:

\begin{verbatim}
>> def square(x: i64) -> i64 { x * x }
>> square(7)
49
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{REPL commands:}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Command & Description \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\texttt{:help} & Show available commands \\
\texttt{:env} & List all active bindings and definitions \\
\texttt{:type\ \textless{}expr\textgreater{}} & Show the inferred type
of an expression \\
\texttt{:bring\ std.math} & Load a standard library module \\
\texttt{:reset} & Clear all session state \\
\texttt{:quit} & Exit the REPL \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{1.5 IDE Setup}\label{ide-setup}

IRIS includes a Language Server Protocol (LSP) server. For Visual Studio
Code:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Install the \texttt{vscode-iris} extension from the
  \texttt{vscode-iris/} folder in the IRIS distribution.
\item
  Open a \texttt{.iris} file --- the extension automatically starts the
  LSP server.
\end{enumerate}

Features provided:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Syntax highlighting
\item
  Hover documentation
\item
  Go to definition
\item
  Error diagnostics (underlines)
\item
  Auto-completion
\item
  Outline view
\item
  Signature help
\item
  Document formatting
\end{itemize}

To start the LSP server manually (for other editors):

\begin{verbatim}
iris lsp
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{1.6 Project Setup and
Testing}\label{project-setup-and-testing}

Modern IRIS development utilizes the built-in package manager and
testing framework:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{Initialize a Project}: Run \texttt{iris\ pkg\ init\ myproject}
  to create a standard project layout:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ExtensionTok{iris}\NormalTok{ pkg init myproject}
\BuiltInTok{cd}\NormalTok{ myproject}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}
\item
  \textbf{Standard Structure}: This generates a \texttt{src/main.iris}
  file and a \texttt{iris.toml} manifest.
\item
  \textbf{Run Tests}: Check that everything is working:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ExtensionTok{iris}\NormalTok{ test}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{1.6 Project Setup and
Testing}\label{project-setup-and-testing-1}

Modern IRIS development utilizes the built-in package manager and
testing framework:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{Initialize a Project}: Run \texttt{iris\ pkg\ init\ myproject}
  to create a standard project layout:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ExtensionTok{iris}\NormalTok{ pkg init myproject}
\BuiltInTok{cd}\NormalTok{ myproject}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}
\item
  \textbf{Standard Structure}: This generates a \texttt{src/main.iris}
  file and a \texttt{iris.toml} manifest.
\item
  \textbf{Run Tests}: Check that everything is working:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ExtensionTok{iris}\NormalTok{ test}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Try It Yourself}\label{try-it-yourself}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Write a program that prints your name.
\item
  Write a program that prints the result of \texttt{100\ *\ 100}.
\item
  Open the REPL and use \texttt{:type} to check the type of
  \texttt{3.14}.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Chapter 2: Values and Types}\label{chapter-2-values-and-types}

\subsection{2.1 Primitive Types}\label{primitive-types}

IRIS has eleven primitive scalar types:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Type & Description & Example \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\texttt{i64} & 64-bit signed integer & \texttt{42}, \texttt{-7} \\
\texttt{i32} & 32-bit signed integer & \texttt{42}, \texttt{-7} \\
\texttt{i8} & 8-bit signed integer & \texttt{42\ to\ i8} \\
\texttt{u8} & 8-bit unsigned integer & \texttt{255\ to\ u8} \\
\texttt{u32} & 32-bit unsigned integer & \texttt{100\ to\ u32} \\
\texttt{u64} & 64-bit unsigned integer & \texttt{100\ to\ u64} \\
\texttt{usize} & Platform pointer size & \texttt{10\ to\ usize} \\
\texttt{f64} & 64-bit floating-point & \texttt{3.14}, \texttt{1.0} \\
\texttt{f32} & 32-bit floating-point & \texttt{3.14\ to\ f32},
\texttt{1.0\ to\ f32} \\
\texttt{bool} & Boolean & \texttt{true}, \texttt{false} \\
\texttt{str} & String (UTF-8) & \texttt{"hello"} \\
\end{longtable}

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Note:} Float literals like \texttt{3.14} are \texttt{f64} by
default. To get an \texttt{f32}, write \texttt{3.14\ to\ f32}.
\end{quote}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{2.2 Bindings: \texttt{val} and
\texttt{var}}{2.2 Bindings: val and var}}\label{bindings-val-and-var}

Use \texttt{val} to create an immutable binding and \texttt{var} for a
mutable one:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ demo() }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ x }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{10}         \CommentTok{// immutable — cannot reassign}
    \KeywordTok{var}\NormalTok{ y }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{20}         \CommentTok{// mutable — can be reassigned}
\NormalTok{    y }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ y }\OperatorTok{+} \DecValTok{5}          \CommentTok{// ok: y is now 25}
\NormalTok{    x }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ y              }\CommentTok{// returns 35}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

If you try to reassign a \texttt{val}, you get a compile error:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ bad() }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ x }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{10}
\NormalTok{    x }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{20}   \CommentTok{// ERROR: cannot assign to immutable binding \textquotesingle{}x\textquotesingle{}}
\NormalTok{    x}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{2.3 Type Inference}\label{type-inference}

IRIS infers types from context, so you rarely need to annotate them
explicitly:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ inferred() }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ a }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{10}        \CommentTok{// inferred as i64}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ b }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{20}        \CommentTok{// inferred as i64}
\NormalTok{    a }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ b             }\CommentTok{// returns i64}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

You can add a type annotation after the binding name with \texttt{:}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ annotated() }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ pi: }\DataTypeTok{f32} \OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{3.14159}
\NormalTok{    pi}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{2.4 Literals and Casts}\label{literals-and-casts}

Integer literals are \texttt{i64} by default. Float literals are
\texttt{f64} by default.

To convert between types, use the \texttt{to} keyword:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ casts() }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{f64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ n: }\DataTypeTok{i64} \OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{42}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ f: }\DataTypeTok{f64} \OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{3.14}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ small: }\DataTypeTok{f32} \OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ f }\KeywordTok{to} \DataTypeTok{f32}     \CommentTok{// f32 {-}\textgreater{} f64}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ also: }\DataTypeTok{f64} \OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ n }\KeywordTok{to} \DataTypeTok{f64}    \CommentTok{// i64 {-}\textgreater{} f64}
\NormalTok{    also }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ big}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Common cast patterns:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ cast\_examples() }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ x: }\DataTypeTok{f64} \OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{9.7} \KeywordTok{to} \DataTypeTok{f64}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ rounded: }\DataTypeTok{i64} \OperatorTok{=} \FunctionTok{floor}\NormalTok{(x) }\KeywordTok{to} \DataTypeTok{i64}     \CommentTok{// 9}
\NormalTok{    rounded}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{2.5 Constants}\label{constants}

Use \texttt{const} to define a module-level constant. Constants are
always typed explicitly:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{const}\NormalTok{ MAX\_SIZE: }\DataTypeTok{i64} \OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{1000}
\KeywordTok{const}\NormalTok{ PI: }\DataTypeTok{f64} \OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{3.14159265358979} \KeywordTok{to} \DataTypeTok{f64}
\KeywordTok{const}\NormalTok{ APP\_NAME: }\DataTypeTok{str} \OperatorTok{=} \StringTok{"MyApp"}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ show\_constants() }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(APP\_NAME);}
\NormalTok{    MAX\_SIZE}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Constants are evaluated at compile time and can be used anywhere in the
module.

\subsection{2.6 Type Aliases}\label{type-aliases}

Use \texttt{type} to create an alias for an existing type:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{type}\NormalTok{ Index }\OperatorTok{=} \DataTypeTok{i64}
\KeywordTok{type}\NormalTok{ Score }\OperatorTok{=} \DataTypeTok{f64}
\KeywordTok{type}\NormalTok{ Name }\OperatorTok{=} \DataTypeTok{str}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ greet(name: Name) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{str}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \FunctionTok{concat}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Hello, "}\NormalTok{, name)}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Type aliases are purely cosmetic --- \texttt{Index} and \texttt{i64} are
the same type to the compiler.

\subsection{2.7 The Type System
Overview}\label{the-type-system-overview}

IRIS uses a structural, nominal type system:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Scalar types} (\texttt{i64}, \texttt{f32}, etc.) are value
  types --- they are copied when assigned.
\item
  \textbf{Records} (structs) are nominal --- two records with the same
  fields but different names are different types.
\item
  \textbf{Enums} (\texttt{choice}) are tagged unions --- each variant
  can carry data.
\item
  \textbf{Options} (\texttt{option\textless{}T\textgreater{}}) wrap a
  value that may or may not be present.
\item
  \textbf{Results} (\texttt{result\textless{}T,\ E\textgreater{}})
  represent success or failure.
\item
  \textbf{Lists} (\texttt{list\textless{}T\textgreater{}}) are dynamic
  arrays.
\item
  \textbf{Maps} (\texttt{map\textless{}K,\ V\textgreater{}}) are hash
  maps.
\item
  \textbf{Tensors}
  (\texttt{tensor\textless{}f32,\ {[}M,\ N{]}\textgreater{}}) are
  N-dimensional arrays for ML.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Try It Yourself}\label{try-it-yourself-1}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Write a function that takes an \texttt{f64} and returns its square
  root as \texttt{f32}. (Hint: use \texttt{sqrt} then cast.)
\item
  Define a constant \texttt{GRAVITY:\ f64} for Earth's gravitational
  acceleration (9.81 m/s²) and write a function that computes the force
  on a given mass.
\item
  Create a type alias \texttt{Celsius\ =\ f64} and write a function that
  converts Celsius to Fahrenheit.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Common Mistakes:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Forgetting that \texttt{3.14} is \texttt{f64}, not \texttt{f32}. If
  you pass it to a function expecting \texttt{f32}, you must write
  \texttt{3.14\ to\ f32}.
\item
  Trying to reassign a \texttt{val} binding. Use \texttt{var} when you
  need mutation.
\item
  Mixing \texttt{i64} and \texttt{f64} in arithmetic without a cast.
  IRIS does not implicitly promote types.
\end{itemize}
\end{quote}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Chapter 3: Functions}\label{chapter-3-functions}

\subsection{3.1 Defining Functions}\label{defining-functions}

The \texttt{def} keyword introduces a function:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ add(a: }\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{, b: }\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
\NormalTok{    a }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ b}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Every parameter has a name and a type separated by \texttt{:}. The
return type follows \texttt{-\textgreater{}}. The function body is
enclosed in \texttt{\{\ \}}.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{3.2 Tail Expressions (No \texttt{return}
Needed)}{3.2 Tail Expressions (No return Needed)}}\label{tail-expressions-no-return-needed}

The last expression in a function body is implicitly returned. This is
the \emph{tail expression}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ multiply(a: }\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{, b: }\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ product }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ a }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ b}
\NormalTok{    product          }\CommentTok{// tail expression — this is the return value}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Most of the time, you do not need an explicit \texttt{return}. Note that
\texttt{val\ product\ =\ a\ *\ b} has no semicolon? That's wrong ---
it's a statement, not a tail expression. Let's be precise:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ multiply(a: }\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{, b: }\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ product }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ a }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ b;   }\CommentTok{// statement — needs semicolon}
\NormalTok{    product                }\CommentTok{// tail expression — no semicolon}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Actually, \texttt{val} bindings do need a semicolon. Let me show the
complete picture:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Statements} (non-tail): need \texttt{;} at the end ---
  \texttt{val\ x\ =\ 5;}, \texttt{print("hi");}, \texttt{f();}
\item
  \textbf{Tail expression} (last line): no \texttt{;} --- this is the
  return value
\end{itemize}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ example() }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ a }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{;          }\CommentTok{// statement: val binding}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ b }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{;          }\CommentTok{// statement: val binding}
    \FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"computing"}\NormalTok{);  }\CommentTok{// statement: side effect call}
\NormalTok{    a }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ b                }\CommentTok{// tail expression: no semicolon, this is returned}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{3.3 Early Return}\label{early-return}

Sometimes you want to return early from a function. Use \texttt{return}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ safe\_divide(a: }\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{, b: }\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ b }\OperatorTok{==} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{ \{}
        \ControlFlowTok{return} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{;}
\NormalTok{    \} }\ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{ \{}
\NormalTok{        a }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ b}
\NormalTok{    \}}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Note:} \texttt{return} exits the function immediately. It must
be inside a statement context (followed by \texttt{;} if not at the end
of a block).
\end{quote}

\subsection{3.4 Recursive Functions}\label{recursive-functions}

Recursion works naturally in IRIS. Here's factorial:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ factorial(n: }\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ n }\OperatorTok{\textless{}=} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{ \{}
        \DecValTok{1}
\NormalTok{    \} }\ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{ \{}
\NormalTok{        n }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ factorial(n }\OperatorTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    \}}
\NormalTok{\}}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ main() }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(factorial(}\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{));}
    \DecValTok{0}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

And Fibonacci:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ fib(n: }\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ n }\OperatorTok{\textless{}=} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{ \{}
\NormalTok{        n}
\NormalTok{    \} }\ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{ \{}
\NormalTok{        fib(n }\OperatorTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ fib(n }\OperatorTok{{-}} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    \}}
\NormalTok{\}}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ main() }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(fib(}\DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{));}
    \DecValTok{0}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Note:} Recursive functions in IRIS are not tail-call optimized
by default. For very deep recursion, prefer iterative approaches.
\end{quote}

Here is an iterative Fibonacci that avoids stack overflow:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ fib\_iter(n: }\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ n }\OperatorTok{\textless{}=} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{ \{}
\NormalTok{        n}
\NormalTok{    \} }\ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{ \{}
        \KeywordTok{var}\NormalTok{ a }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{;}
        \KeywordTok{var}\NormalTok{ b }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{;}
        \KeywordTok{var}\NormalTok{ i }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{;}
        \ControlFlowTok{while}\NormalTok{ i }\OperatorTok{\textless{}=}\NormalTok{ n \{}
            \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ tmp }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ a }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ b;}
\NormalTok{            a }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ b;}
\NormalTok{            b }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ tmp;}
\NormalTok{            i }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ i }\OperatorTok{+} \DecValTok{1}
\NormalTok{        \}}
\NormalTok{        b}
\NormalTok{    \}}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{3.5 Public Functions}\label{public-functions}

By default, functions are private to their module. Use \texttt{pub\ def}
to make a function accessible from other modules:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{// In mylib.iris}
\KeywordTok{pub} \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ greet(name: }\DataTypeTok{str}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{str}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \FunctionTok{concat}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Hello, "}\NormalTok{, name)}
\NormalTok{\}}

\CommentTok{// Private helper — not exported}
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ helper() }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \DecValTok{42}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

When another file brings in \texttt{mylib}, only \texttt{greet} is
accessible.

\subsection{3.6 Default Parameters}\label{default-parameters}

Functions can have default parameter values:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ repeat\_char(c: }\DataTypeTok{str}\NormalTok{, n: }\DataTypeTok{i64} \OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{str}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \FunctionTok{repeat}\NormalTok{(c, n)}
\NormalTok{\}}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ main() }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(repeat\_char(}\StringTok{"{-}"}\NormalTok{));      }\CommentTok{// uses default n=3: "{-}{-}{-}"}
    \FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(repeat\_char(}\StringTok{"*"}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{));   }\CommentTok{// overrides: "*****"}
    \DecValTok{0}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{3.7 Functions as First-Class
Values}\label{functions-as-first-class-values}

Functions can be stored and passed around using function types. The type
\texttt{(i64,\ i64)\ -\textgreater{}\ i64} describes a function that
takes two \texttt{i64} arguments and returns an \texttt{i64}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ apply(f: (}\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{, x: }\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
\NormalTok{    f(x)}
\NormalTok{\}}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ double(x: }\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
\NormalTok{    x }\OperatorTok{*} \DecValTok{2}
\NormalTok{\}}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ main() }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \KeywordTok{val} \DataTypeTok{result} \OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ apply(double, }\DecValTok{21}\NormalTok{);}
    \FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{result}\NormalTok{);   }\CommentTok{// prints 42}
    \DecValTok{0}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{Try It Yourself}\label{try-it-yourself-2}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Write a recursive function
  \texttt{power(base:\ i64,\ exp:\ i64)\ -\textgreater{}\ i64} that
  computes \texttt{base} raised to \texttt{exp}.
\item
  Write a function
  \texttt{clamp\_score(score:\ f32,\ lo:\ f32\ =\ 0.0,\ hi:\ f32\ =\ 100.0)\ -\textgreater{}\ f32}
  with default bounds.
\item
  Write a function \texttt{sum\_to(n:\ i64)\ -\textgreater{}\ i64} using
  a \texttt{while} loop (we will cover loops in chapter 4, but give it a
  try).
\end{enumerate}

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Common Mistakes:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Forgetting the semicolon after non-tail statements inside a function.
  \texttt{print("hi")} without \texttt{;} will be parsed as the tail
  expression, making the function return type \texttt{unit} instead of
  what you intended.
\item
  Naming a function parameter the same as a built-in (like \texttt{len}
  or \texttt{print}). This shadows the built-in.
\item
  Confusing \texttt{return\ expr} (early exit) with just \texttt{expr}
  (tail expression). Both work, but \texttt{return} at the tail position
  is redundant and slightly less idiomatic.
\end{itemize}
\end{quote}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Chapter 4: Control Flow}\label{chapter-4-control-flow}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{4.1
\texttt{if\ /\ else}}{4.1 if / else}}\label{if-else}

The \texttt{if} expression in IRIS \textbf{always} requires an
\texttt{else} branch:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ abs\_val(x: }\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ x }\OperatorTok{\textless{}} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{ \{ }\DecValTok{0} \OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ x \} }\ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{ \{ x \}}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{if} is an expression, meaning it produces a value. Both branches
must produce the same type:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ classify(n: }\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{str}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ n }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{ \{}
        \StringTok{"positive"}
\NormalTok{    \} }\ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{ \{}
        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ n }\OperatorTok{\textless{}} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{ \{}
            \StringTok{"negative"}
\NormalTok{        \} }\ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{ \{}
            \StringTok{"zero"}
\NormalTok{        \}}
\NormalTok{    \}}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

You can use \texttt{if} inline in expressions:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ max\_of(a: }\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{, b: }\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ bigger }\OperatorTok{=} \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ a }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ b \{ a \} }\ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{ \{ b \};}
\NormalTok{    bigger}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Common Mistakes:} Omitting \texttt{else}. This is the single
most common error for beginners. IRIS requires \texttt{else} because
\texttt{if} produces a value --- without \texttt{else}, the type of the
expression is undefined.
\end{quote}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{4.2 \texttt{while}
Loops}{4.2 while Loops}}\label{while-loops}

\texttt{while} loops repeat as long as a condition is true:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ count\_down(from: }\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \KeywordTok{var}\NormalTok{ n }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ from;}
    \ControlFlowTok{while}\NormalTok{ n }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{ \{}
        \FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(n);}
\NormalTok{        n }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ n }\OperatorTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1}
\NormalTok{    \}}
    \DecValTok{0}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Compute the sum of 1..100:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ sum\_one\_to\_hundred() }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \KeywordTok{var}\NormalTok{ total }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{;}
    \KeywordTok{var}\NormalTok{ i }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{;}
    \ControlFlowTok{while}\NormalTok{ i }\OperatorTok{\textless{}=} \DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{ \{}
\NormalTok{        total }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ total }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ i;}
\NormalTok{        i }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ i }\OperatorTok{+} \DecValTok{1}
\NormalTok{    \}}
\NormalTok{    total}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{4.3 \texttt{for} Range
Loops}{4.3 for Range Loops}}\label{for-range-loops}

The \texttt{for\ i\ in\ start..end} loop iterates over the half-open
range \texttt{{[}start,\ end)}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ print\_range() }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ i }\ControlFlowTok{in} \DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{..}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{ \{}
        \FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(i)}
\NormalTok{    \}}
    \DecValTok{0}
\NormalTok{\}}
\CommentTok{// prints: 0 1 2 3 4}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

For loops are clean and idiomatic when you need a counter:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ sum\_range(n: }\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \KeywordTok{var}\NormalTok{ total }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{;}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ i }\ControlFlowTok{in} \DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{..}\NormalTok{n }\OperatorTok{+} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{ \{}
\NormalTok{        total }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ total }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ i}
\NormalTok{    \}}
\NormalTok{    total}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Note:} The range \texttt{start..end} is exclusive of
\texttt{end}. To include \texttt{end}, write \texttt{start..end\ +\ 1}.
\end{quote}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{4.4 \texttt{loop} with
\texttt{break}}{4.4 loop with break}}\label{loop-with-break}

The \texttt{loop} construct runs forever until you explicitly
\texttt{break}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ find\_first\_even(start: }\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \KeywordTok{var}\NormalTok{ n }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ start;}
    \KeywordTok{var} \DataTypeTok{result} \OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{;}
    \ControlFlowTok{loop}\NormalTok{ \{}
        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (n }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ (n }\OperatorTok{/} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{*} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{==} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{ \{}
            \DataTypeTok{result} \OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ n;}
            \ControlFlowTok{break}
\NormalTok{        \} }\ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{ \{}
\NormalTok{            n }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ n }\OperatorTok{+} \DecValTok{1}
\NormalTok{        \}}
\NormalTok{    \}}
    \DataTypeTok{result}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{4.5 \texttt{break} and
\texttt{continue}}{4.5 break and continue}}\label{break-and-continue}

Inside any loop, \texttt{break} exits the loop and \texttt{continue}
skips the rest of the current iteration:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ skip\_multiples\_of\_3(limit: }\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \KeywordTok{var}\NormalTok{ printed }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{;}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ i }\ControlFlowTok{in} \DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{..}\NormalTok{limit }\OperatorTok{+} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{ \{}
        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (i }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ (i }\OperatorTok{/} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{*} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{==} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{ \{}
            \ControlFlowTok{continue}
\NormalTok{        \} }\ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{ \{}
            \FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(i);}
\NormalTok{            printed }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ printed }\OperatorTok{+} \DecValTok{1}
\NormalTok{        \}}
\NormalTok{    \}}
\NormalTok{    printed}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{4.6 Nested Loops}\label{nested-loops}

Loops can be nested freely:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ multiplication\_table(n: }\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ i }\ControlFlowTok{in} \DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{..}\NormalTok{n }\OperatorTok{+} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{ \{}
        \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ j }\ControlFlowTok{in} \DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{..}\NormalTok{n }\OperatorTok{+} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{ \{}
            \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ product }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ i }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ j;}
            \FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(product)}
\NormalTok{        \}}
\NormalTok{    \}}
    \DecValTok{0}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{4.7 Logical Operators}\label{logical-operators}

IRIS supports short-circuit logical operators:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{\&\&} --- logical AND (short-circuits: if left is false, right
  is not evaluated)
\item
  \texttt{\textbar{}\textbar{}} --- logical OR (short-circuits: if left
  is true, right is not evaluated)
\end{itemize}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ check(x: }\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{, y: }\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{bool}\NormalTok{ \{}
\NormalTok{    x }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}} \DecValTok{0} \OperatorTok{\&\&}\NormalTok{ y }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}} \DecValTok{0}
\NormalTok{\}}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ either\_positive(x: }\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{, y: }\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{bool}\NormalTok{ \{}
\NormalTok{    x }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}} \DecValTok{0} \OperatorTok{||}\NormalTok{ y }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}} \DecValTok{0}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{4.8 For-Each Loops}\label{for-each-loops}

IRIS supports iterating directly over collections like lists, ranges, or
arrays:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ main() }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ items }\OperatorTok{=} \DataTypeTok{list}\NormalTok{();}
    \FunctionTok{push}\NormalTok{(items, }\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{); }\FunctionTok{push}\NormalTok{(items, }\DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{); }\FunctionTok{push}\NormalTok{(items, }\DecValTok{30}\NormalTok{);}
    
    \CommentTok{// Iterate over elements directly}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ x }\ControlFlowTok{in}\NormalTok{ items \{}
        \FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{to\_str}\NormalTok{(x))}
\NormalTok{    \}}
    \DecValTok{0}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{4.9 Tuple Destructuring}\label{tuple-destructuring}

You can bind multiple values at once by destructuring tuples:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ get\_coords() }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ (}\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{, }\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{) \{}
\NormalTok{    (}\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{200}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\}}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ main() }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ (x, y) }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ get\_coords();}
    \FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{concat}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"X: "}\NormalTok{, }\FunctionTok{to\_str}\NormalTok{(x)));}
    \FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{concat}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Y: "}\NormalTok{, }\FunctionTok{to\_str}\NormalTok{(y)));}
    \DecValTok{0}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{4.10 Keyword Operators (\texttt{and},
\texttt{or},
\texttt{not})}{4.10 Keyword Operators (and, or, not)}}\label{keyword-operators-and-or-not}

In addition to \texttt{\&\&}, \texttt{\textbar{}\textbar{}}, and
\texttt{!}, IRIS supports readable keyword operators:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ eligible(age: }\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{, registered: }\DataTypeTok{bool}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{bool}\NormalTok{ \{}
\NormalTok{    age }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}=} \DecValTok{18} \KeywordTok{and}\NormalTok{ registered}
\NormalTok{\}}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ can\_enter(has\_ticket: }\DataTypeTok{bool}\NormalTok{, is\_vip: }\DataTypeTok{bool}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{bool}\NormalTok{ \{}
\NormalTok{    has\_ticket }\KeywordTok{or}\NormalTok{ is\_vip}
\NormalTok{\}}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ is\_minor(age: }\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{bool}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \KeywordTok{not}\NormalTok{ (age }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}=} \DecValTok{18}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{4.8 For-Each Loops}\label{for-each-loops-1}

IRIS supports iterating directly over collections like lists, ranges, or
arrays:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ main() }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ items }\OperatorTok{=} \DataTypeTok{list}\NormalTok{();}
    \FunctionTok{push}\NormalTok{(items, }\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{); }\FunctionTok{push}\NormalTok{(items, }\DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{); }\FunctionTok{push}\NormalTok{(items, }\DecValTok{30}\NormalTok{);}
    
    \CommentTok{// Iterate over elements directly}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ x }\ControlFlowTok{in}\NormalTok{ items \{}
        \FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{to\_str}\NormalTok{(x))}
\NormalTok{    \}}
    \DecValTok{0}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{4.9 Tuple Destructuring}\label{tuple-destructuring-1}

You can bind multiple values at once by destructuring tuples:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ get\_coords() }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ (}\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{, }\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{) \{}
\NormalTok{    (}\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{200}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\}}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ main() }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ (x, y) }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ get\_coords();}
    \FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{concat}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"X: "}\NormalTok{, }\FunctionTok{to\_str}\NormalTok{(x)));}
    \FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{concat}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Y: "}\NormalTok{, }\FunctionTok{to\_str}\NormalTok{(y)));}
    \DecValTok{0}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{4.10 Keyword Operators (\texttt{and},
\texttt{or},
\texttt{not})}{4.10 Keyword Operators (and, or, not)}}\label{keyword-operators-and-or-not-1}

In addition to \texttt{\&\&}, \texttt{\textbar{}\textbar{}}, and
\texttt{!}, IRIS supports readable keyword operators:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ eligible(age: }\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{, registered: }\DataTypeTok{bool}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{bool}\NormalTok{ \{}
\NormalTok{    age }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}=} \DecValTok{18} \KeywordTok{and}\NormalTok{ registered}
\NormalTok{\}}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ can\_enter(has\_ticket: }\DataTypeTok{bool}\NormalTok{, is\_vip: }\DataTypeTok{bool}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{bool}\NormalTok{ \{}
\NormalTok{    has\_ticket }\KeywordTok{or}\NormalTok{ is\_vip}
\NormalTok{\}}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ is\_minor(age: }\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{bool}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \KeywordTok{not}\NormalTok{ (age }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}=} \DecValTok{18}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{Try It Yourself}\label{try-it-yourself-3}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Write a function \texttt{is\_prime(n:\ i64)\ -\textgreater{}\ bool}
  using a \texttt{for} loop and \texttt{break}.
\item
  Write a function
  \texttt{collatz\_steps(n:\ i64)\ -\textgreater{}\ i64} that counts how
  many steps the Collatz sequence takes to reach 1 (if n is even, divide
  by 2; if odd, multiply by 3 and add 1).
\item
  Write a function that prints a triangle of asterisks of height
  \texttt{h} (row 1 has one \texttt{*}, row 2 has two, etc.) using
  nested \texttt{for} loops.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Common Mistakes:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Writing \texttt{if\ cond\ \{\ body\ \}} without an \texttt{else}.
  Always add \texttt{else\ \{\ ...\ \}}, even if it just returns
  \texttt{0}.
\item
  Off-by-one in ranges. \texttt{for\ i\ in\ 0..n} gives \texttt{n}
  iterations (0 through n-1), not n+1.
\item
  Using \texttt{\%} for modulo --- that is correct in IRIS. Do not look
  for \texttt{mod} keyword.
\end{itemize}
\end{quote}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Chapter 5: Data Structures}\label{chapter-5-data-structures}

\subsection{5.1 Records}\label{records}

Records are named collections of fields, similar to structs in C or
Rust:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{record}\NormalTok{ Point \{}
\NormalTok{    x: }\DataTypeTok{f64}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    y: }\DataTypeTok{f64}
\NormalTok{\}}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ make\_point(x: }\DataTypeTok{f64}\NormalTok{, y: }\DataTypeTok{f64}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Point \{}
\NormalTok{    Point \{ x: x, y: y \}}
\NormalTok{\}}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ distance(p: Point) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{f64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \FunctionTok{sqrt}\NormalTok{((p}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{x }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ p}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{x) }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ (p}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{y }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ p}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{y)) }\KeywordTok{to} \DataTypeTok{f64}
\NormalTok{\}}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ main() }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ p }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Point \{ x: }\FloatTok{3.0} \KeywordTok{to} \DataTypeTok{f64}\NormalTok{, y: }\FloatTok{4.0} \KeywordTok{to} \DataTypeTok{f64}\NormalTok{ \};}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ d }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ distance(p);}
    \FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(d);   }\CommentTok{// 5.0}
    \DecValTok{0}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Records can contain any types, including other records:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{record}\NormalTok{ Color \{}
\NormalTok{    r: }\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    g: }\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    b: }\DataTypeTok{f32}
\NormalTok{\}}

\KeywordTok{record}\NormalTok{ Pixel \{}
\NormalTok{    x: }\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    y: }\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    color: Color}
\NormalTok{\}}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ make\_red\_pixel(x: }\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{, y: }\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Pixel \{}
\NormalTok{    Pixel \{}
\NormalTok{        x: x,}
\NormalTok{        y: y,}
\NormalTok{        color: Color \{ r: }\FloatTok{1.0}\NormalTok{, g: }\FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{, b: }\FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{ \}}
\NormalTok{    \}}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{5.2 Enums
(\texttt{choice})}{5.2 Enums (choice)}}\label{enums-choice}

Enums define a type with a fixed set of variants. Use \texttt{choice} to
declare them:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{choice}\NormalTok{ Direction \{}
\NormalTok{    North,}
\NormalTok{    South,}
\NormalTok{    East,}
\NormalTok{    West}
\NormalTok{\}}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ opposite(d: Direction) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Direction \{}
    \ControlFlowTok{when}\NormalTok{ d \{}
\NormalTok{        Direction}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{North }\OperatorTok{=\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Direction}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{South,}
\NormalTok{        Direction}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{South }\OperatorTok{=\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Direction}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{North,}
\NormalTok{        Direction}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{East  }\OperatorTok{=\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Direction}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{West,}
\NormalTok{        Direction}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{West  }\OperatorTok{=\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Direction}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{East}
\NormalTok{    \}}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{5.3 Pattern Matching with
\texttt{when}}{5.3 Pattern Matching with when}}\label{pattern-matching-with-when}

The \texttt{when} expression matches a value against its variants:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{choice}\NormalTok{ Shape \{}
\NormalTok{    Circle,}
\NormalTok{    Square,}
\NormalTok{    Triangle}
\NormalTok{\}}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ sides(s: Shape) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \ControlFlowTok{when}\NormalTok{ s \{}
\NormalTok{        Shape}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{Circle   }\OperatorTok{=\textgreater{}} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{        Shape}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{Square   }\OperatorTok{=\textgreater{}} \DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{        Shape}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{Triangle }\OperatorTok{=\textgreater{}} \DecValTok{3}
\NormalTok{    \}}
\NormalTok{\}}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ describe(s: Shape) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{str}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \ControlFlowTok{when}\NormalTok{ s \{}
\NormalTok{        Shape}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{Circle   }\OperatorTok{=\textgreater{}} \StringTok{"A round shape with no sides"}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{        Shape}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{Square   }\OperatorTok{=\textgreater{}} \StringTok{"A four{-}sided shape"}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{        Shape}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{Triangle }\OperatorTok{=\textgreater{}} \StringTok{"A three{-}sided shape"}
\NormalTok{    \}}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{5.4 Tuples}\label{tuples}

Tuples are ordered collections of values with potentially different
types. Access elements with \texttt{.0}, \texttt{.1}, \texttt{.2}, etc.:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ make\_pair(a: }\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{, b: }\DataTypeTok{str}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ (}\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{, }\DataTypeTok{str}\NormalTok{) \{}
\NormalTok{    (a, b)}
\NormalTok{\}}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ main() }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ pair }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ make\_pair(}\DecValTok{42}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"hello"}\NormalTok{);}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ num }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ pair}\OperatorTok{.}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{;   }\CommentTok{// 42}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ text }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ pair}\OperatorTok{.}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{;  }\CommentTok{// "hello"}
    \FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(num);}
    \FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(text);}
    \DecValTok{0}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Tuples are great for returning multiple values from a function:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ min\_max(a: }\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{, b: }\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{, c: }\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ (}\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{, }\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{) \{}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ lo }\OperatorTok{=} \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ a }\OperatorTok{\textless{}}\NormalTok{ b \{ }\ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ a }\OperatorTok{\textless{}}\NormalTok{ c \{ a \} }\ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{ \{ c \} \} }\ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{ \{ }\ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ b }\OperatorTok{\textless{}}\NormalTok{ c \{ b \} }\ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{ \{ c \} \};}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ hi }\OperatorTok{=} \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ a }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ b \{ }\ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ a }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ c \{ a \} }\ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{ \{ c \} \} }\ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{ \{ }\ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ b }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ c \{ b \} }\ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{ \{ c \} \};}
\NormalTok{    (lo, hi)}
\NormalTok{\}}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ main() }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \KeywordTok{val} \DataTypeTok{result} \OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ min\_max(}\DecValTok{7}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{9}\NormalTok{);}
    \FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{result}\OperatorTok{.}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{);   }\CommentTok{// 2}
    \FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\DataTypeTok{result}\OperatorTok{.}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{);   }\CommentTok{// 9}
    \DecValTok{0}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{5.5 Fixed Arrays}\label{fixed-arrays}

Arrays have a compile-time fixed size. The type \texttt{{[}T;\ N{]}} is
an array of \texttt{N} elements of type \texttt{T}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ sum\_array() }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ nums: [}\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{; }\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ [}\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{30}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{40}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{50}\NormalTok{];}
    \KeywordTok{var}\NormalTok{ total }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{;}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ i }\ControlFlowTok{in} \DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{..}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{ \{}
\NormalTok{        total }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ total }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ nums[i]}
\NormalTok{    \}}
\NormalTok{    total}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Arrays support element assignment (they are mutable by default):

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ zero\_fill(size: }\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ arr: [}\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{; }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ [}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{];}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ i }\ControlFlowTok{in} \DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{..}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{ \{}
\NormalTok{        arr[i] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ i }\OperatorTok{*} \DecValTok{2}
\NormalTok{    \}}
\NormalTok{    arr[}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{]   }\CommentTok{// returns 6}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{5.6 Dynamic Lists}\label{dynamic-lists}

Lists are resizable arrays. \texttt{list()} creates an empty
\texttt{list\textless{}i64\textgreater{}}. Use
\texttt{list\textless{}T\textgreater{}()} for other element types:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ build\_list() }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ nums }\OperatorTok{=} \DataTypeTok{list}\NormalTok{();           }\CommentTok{// list\textless{}i64\textgreater{}}
    \FunctionTok{push}\NormalTok{(nums, }\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{);}
    \FunctionTok{push}\NormalTok{(nums, }\DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{);}
    \FunctionTok{push}\NormalTok{(nums, }\DecValTok{30}\NormalTok{);}
    \FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{list\_len}\NormalTok{(nums));       }\CommentTok{// 3}
    \FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{list\_get}\NormalTok{(nums, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{));    }\CommentTok{// 20}
    \FunctionTok{list\_len}\NormalTok{(nums)}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

List operations:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ list\_demo() }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ items }\OperatorTok{=} \DataTypeTok{list}\NormalTok{();}
    \FunctionTok{push}\NormalTok{(items, }\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{);}
    \FunctionTok{push}\NormalTok{(items, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{);}
    \FunctionTok{push}\NormalTok{(items, }\DecValTok{8}\NormalTok{);}
    \FunctionTok{push}\NormalTok{(items, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{);}

    \CommentTok{// Access by index}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ first }\OperatorTok{=} \FunctionTok{list\_get}\NormalTok{(items, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{);     }\CommentTok{// 5}

    \CommentTok{// Modify by index}
    \FunctionTok{list\_set}\NormalTok{(items, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{);}

    \CommentTok{// Length}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ n }\OperatorTok{=} \FunctionTok{list\_len}\NormalTok{(items);       }\CommentTok{// 4}

    \CommentTok{// Pop from end}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ last }\OperatorTok{=} \FunctionTok{list\_pop}\NormalTok{(items);    }\CommentTok{// returns last element}

\NormalTok{    n}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

For lists of strings, specify the type explicitly:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ string\_list() }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ names }\OperatorTok{=} \DataTypeTok{list}\NormalTok{();}
    \FunctionTok{push}\NormalTok{(names, }\StringTok{"Alice"}\NormalTok{);}
    \FunctionTok{push}\NormalTok{(names, }\StringTok{"Bob"}\NormalTok{);}
    \FunctionTok{push}\NormalTok{(names, }\StringTok{"Charlie"}\NormalTok{);}
    \FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{list\_get}\NormalTok{(names, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{));   }\CommentTok{// Alice}
    \FunctionTok{list\_len}\NormalTok{(names)}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{5.7 Maps}\label{maps}

Maps store key-value pairs. The
\texttt{map\textless{}K,\ V\textgreater{}} type associates keys of type
\texttt{K} with values of type \texttt{V}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ word\_count() }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ counts }\OperatorTok{=} \DataTypeTok{map}\NormalTok{();}
    \FunctionTok{map\_set}\NormalTok{(counts, }\StringTok{"apple"}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{);}
    \FunctionTok{map\_set}\NormalTok{(counts, }\StringTok{"banana"}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{7}\NormalTok{);}
    \FunctionTok{map\_set}\NormalTok{(counts, }\StringTok{"cherry"}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{);}

    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ found }\OperatorTok{=} \FunctionTok{map\_get}\NormalTok{(counts, }\StringTok{"banana"}\NormalTok{);}
    \ControlFlowTok{if} \FunctionTok{is\_some}\NormalTok{(found) \{}
        \FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(unwrap(found))   }\CommentTok{// prints 7}
\NormalTok{    \} }\ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{ \{}
        \FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    \}}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Map operations:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.4583}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.5417}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Operation
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Description
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\texttt{map\_set(m,\ k,\ v)} & Insert or update key \texttt{k} with
value \texttt{v} \\
\texttt{map\_get(m,\ k)} & Returns
\texttt{option\textless{}V\textgreater{}} --- some if key exists, none
if not \\
\texttt{map\_contains(m,\ k)} & Returns \texttt{bool} --- true if key
exists \\
\texttt{map\_remove(m,\ k)} & Remove key \texttt{k} \\
\texttt{map\_len(m)} & Number of entries \\
\texttt{map\_keys(m)} & Returns
\texttt{list\textless{}str\textgreater{}} of all keys \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{5.8 Options}\label{options}

Options represent a value that may or may not be present.
\texttt{option\textless{}T\textgreater{}} is either \texttt{some(v)}
(contains a value) or \texttt{none} (no value):

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ safe\_head(lst: }\DataTypeTok{list}\OperatorTok{\textless{}}\DataTypeTok{i64}\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{option}\OperatorTok{\textless{}}\DataTypeTok{i64}\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \ControlFlowTok{if} \FunctionTok{list\_len}\NormalTok{(lst) }\OperatorTok{==} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{ \{}
        \ConstantTok{none}
\NormalTok{    \} }\ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{ \{}
        \FunctionTok{some}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{list\_get}\NormalTok{(lst, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{    \}}
\NormalTok{\}}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ main() }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ lst }\OperatorTok{=} \DataTypeTok{list}\NormalTok{();}
    \FunctionTok{push}\NormalTok{(lst, }\DecValTok{42}\NormalTok{);}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ head }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ safe\_head(lst);}
    \ControlFlowTok{if} \FunctionTok{is\_some}\NormalTok{(head) \{}
        \FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(unwrap(head))   }\CommentTok{// prints 42}
\NormalTok{    \} }\ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{ \{}
        \FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    \}}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Common option functions:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Function & Description \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\texttt{some(v)} & Wrap value \texttt{v} in an option \\
\texttt{none} & The absent option value \\
\texttt{is\_some(opt)} & Returns \texttt{bool} --- is the option
present? \\
\texttt{unwrap(opt)} & Extract the value (panics if none) \\
\end{longtable}

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Note:} \texttt{find(s,\ sub)} returns
\texttt{option\textless{}i64\textgreater{}}. Always use
\texttt{is\_some()} to check before calling \texttt{unwrap()}. Do not
compare the result with \texttt{\textless{}\ 0} --- that is for C's
\texttt{strstr}, not IRIS.
\end{quote}

\subsection{5.9 Results}\label{results}

Results represent either success or failure.
\texttt{result\textless{}T,\ E\textgreater{}} is either \texttt{ok(v)}
(success with value \texttt{v}) or \texttt{err(e)} (failure with error
\texttt{e}):

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ parse\_age(s: }\DataTypeTok{str}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{result}\OperatorTok{\textless{}}\DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{, }\DataTypeTok{str}\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ parsed }\OperatorTok{=} \FunctionTok{parse\_i64}\NormalTok{(s);}
    \ControlFlowTok{if} \FunctionTok{is\_some}\NormalTok{(parsed) \{}
        \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ age }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ unwrap(parsed);}
        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ age }\OperatorTok{\textless{}} \DecValTok{0} \OperatorTok{||}\NormalTok{ age }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}} \DecValTok{150}\NormalTok{ \{}
            \FunctionTok{err}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"age out of range"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{        \} }\ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{ \{}
            \FunctionTok{ok}\NormalTok{(age)}
\NormalTok{        \}}
\NormalTok{    \} }\ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{ \{}
        \FunctionTok{err}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"not a number"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    \}}
\NormalTok{\}}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ main() }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ r }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ parse\_age(}\StringTok{"25"}\NormalTok{);}
    \ControlFlowTok{if} \FunctionTok{is\_ok}\NormalTok{(r) \{}
        \FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(unwrap(r))   }\CommentTok{// 25}
\NormalTok{    \} }\ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{ \{}
        \FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"error"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    \}}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{5.10 Deques}\label{deques}

Deques are double-ended queues supporting efficient push/pop at both
ends:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ deque\_demo() }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ dq }\OperatorTok{=} \FunctionTok{deque\_new}\NormalTok{();}
    \FunctionTok{deque\_push\_back}\NormalTok{(dq, }\DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{);}
    \FunctionTok{deque\_push\_front}\NormalTok{(dq, }\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{);}
    \FunctionTok{deque\_push\_back}\NormalTok{(dq, }\DecValTok{30}\NormalTok{);}
    
    \FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{to\_str}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{deque\_pop\_front}\NormalTok{(dq))); }\CommentTok{// 10}
    \FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{to\_str}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{deque\_pop\_back}\NormalTok{(dq)));  }\CommentTok{// 30}
    \DecValTok{0}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{5.11 BitSets}\label{bitsets}

BitSets provide compact, high-performance bit-array collections:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ bitset\_demo() }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ bs }\OperatorTok{=} \FunctionTok{bitset\_new}\NormalTok{();}
    \FunctionTok{bitset\_set}\NormalTok{(bs, }\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{, }\ConstantTok{true}\NormalTok{);}
    \FunctionTok{bitset\_set}\NormalTok{(bs, }\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{, }\ConstantTok{true}\NormalTok{);}
    
    \FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{to\_str}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{bitset\_get}\NormalTok{(bs, }\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{)));   }\CommentTok{// true}
    \FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{to\_str}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{bitset\_get}\NormalTok{(bs, }\DecValTok{7}\NormalTok{)));   }\CommentTok{// false}
    \FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{to\_str}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{bitset\_count}\NormalTok{(bs)));    }\CommentTok{// 2}
    \DecValTok{0}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{5.12 Mutexes}\label{mutexes}

Mutexes provide thread-safe mutual exclusion for shared state:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ mutex\_demo() }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ m }\OperatorTok{=} \DataTypeTok{mutex}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{42}\NormalTok{);}
    \CommentTok{// Lock and modify inside spawn}
    \KeywordTok{spawn}\NormalTok{ \{}
        \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ val\_ref }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ m; }\CommentTok{// Reference to same mutex}
        \CommentTok{// Locks are managed safely by built{-}ins}
        \DecValTok{0}
\NormalTok{    \};}
    \DecValTok{0}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{5.10 Deques}\label{deques-1}

Deques are double-ended queues supporting efficient push/pop at both
ends:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ deque\_demo() }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ dq }\OperatorTok{=} \FunctionTok{deque\_new}\NormalTok{();}
    \FunctionTok{deque\_push\_back}\NormalTok{(dq, }\DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{);}
    \FunctionTok{deque\_push\_front}\NormalTok{(dq, }\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{);}
    \FunctionTok{deque\_push\_back}\NormalTok{(dq, }\DecValTok{30}\NormalTok{);}
    
    \FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{to\_str}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{deque\_pop\_front}\NormalTok{(dq))); }\CommentTok{// 10}
    \FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{to\_str}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{deque\_pop\_back}\NormalTok{(dq)));  }\CommentTok{// 30}
    \DecValTok{0}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{5.11 BitSets}\label{bitsets-1}

BitSets provide compact, high-performance bit-array collections:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ bitset\_demo() }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ bs }\OperatorTok{=} \FunctionTok{bitset\_new}\NormalTok{();}
    \FunctionTok{bitset\_set}\NormalTok{(bs, }\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{, }\ConstantTok{true}\NormalTok{);}
    \FunctionTok{bitset\_set}\NormalTok{(bs, }\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{, }\ConstantTok{true}\NormalTok{);}
    
    \FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{to\_str}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{bitset\_get}\NormalTok{(bs, }\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{)));   }\CommentTok{// true}
    \FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{to\_str}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{bitset\_get}\NormalTok{(bs, }\DecValTok{7}\NormalTok{)));   }\CommentTok{// false}
    \FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{to\_str}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{bitset\_count}\NormalTok{(bs)));    }\CommentTok{// 2}
    \DecValTok{0}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{5.12 Mutexes}\label{mutexes-1}

Mutexes provide thread-safe mutual exclusion for shared state:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ mutex\_demo() }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ m }\OperatorTok{=} \DataTypeTok{mutex}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{42}\NormalTok{);}
    \CommentTok{// Lock and modify inside spawn}
    \KeywordTok{spawn}\NormalTok{ \{}
        \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ val\_ref }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ m; }\CommentTok{// Reference to same mutex}
        \CommentTok{// Locks are managed safely by built{-}ins}
        \DecValTok{0}
\NormalTok{    \};}
    \DecValTok{0}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{Try It Yourself}\label{try-it-yourself-4}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Define a
  \texttt{record\ Rectangle\ \{\ width:\ f64,\ height:\ f64\ \}} and
  write functions \texttt{area(r:\ Rectangle)\ -\textgreater{}\ f64} and
  \texttt{perimeter(r:\ Rectangle)\ -\textgreater{}\ f64}.
\item
  Define a
  \texttt{choice\ Season\ \{\ Spring,\ Summer,\ Autumn,\ Winter\ \}} and
  write a function that returns the average temperature for each season.
\item
  Write a function that takes a
  \texttt{list\textless{}i64\textgreater{}} and returns a tuple
  \texttt{(i64,\ i64)} containing the minimum and maximum values.
\item
  Write a function
  \texttt{lookup(m:\ map\textless{}str,\ i64\textgreater{},\ key:\ str,\ default:\ i64)\ -\textgreater{}\ i64}
  that returns the map value or a default if the key is missing.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Common Mistakes:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Calling \texttt{unwrap()} on a \texttt{none} option. Always check
  \texttt{is\_some()} first.
\item
  Forgetting that \texttt{list()} creates a
  \texttt{list\textless{}i64\textgreater{}}. For other types, use
  \texttt{list\textless{}str\textgreater{}()}, etc.
\item
  Mutating a \texttt{val}-bound list. Lists are reference types --- even
  a \texttt{val} binding can mutate the list's contents. Use
  \texttt{val} when the binding itself won't change (you won't point it
  at a different list), and \texttt{var} when you might reassign the
  binding to a completely new list.
\end{itemize}
\end{quote}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Chapter 6: Traits and
Generics}\label{chapter-6-traits-and-generics}

IRIS features a robust type system that supports traits and generics,
allowing for clean code reuse, static polymorphism, and generic
programming.

\subsection{6.1 Trait Declarations}\label{trait-declarations}

A \textbf{trait} defines a contract or interface of method signatures
that types must satisfy:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{trait}\NormalTok{ Printable \{}
    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ to\_string(self: Self) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{str}
\NormalTok{\}}

\KeywordTok{trait}\NormalTok{ Comparable \{}
    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ compare(self: Self, other: Self) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The keyword \texttt{Self} (capitalized) inside a trait definition
represents the type that will implement the trait.

\subsection{6.2 Implementing Traits}\label{implementing-traits}

Use the \texttt{impl} keyword to implement a trait for a concrete record
type:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{record}\NormalTok{ Point \{}
\NormalTok{    x: }\DataTypeTok{f64}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    y: }\DataTypeTok{f64}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{\}}

\KeywordTok{impl}\NormalTok{ Printable }\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ Point \{}
    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ to\_string(self: Point) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{str}\NormalTok{ \{}
        \FunctionTok{format}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"(\{\}, \{\})"}\NormalTok{, self}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{x, self}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{y)}
\NormalTok{    \}}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Once a trait is implemented, its methods can be called on instances of
that type:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ main() }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \KeywordTok{val}\NormalTok{ p }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Point \{ x: }\FloatTok{3.5}\NormalTok{, y: }\OperatorTok{{-}}\FloatTok{2.0}\NormalTok{ \};}
    \FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(p}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{to\_string());}
    \DecValTok{0}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{6.3 Generic Functions}\label{generic-functions}

Generic functions declare type parameters inside square brackets
\texttt{{[}T{]}}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ identity[T](x: T) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ T \{}
\NormalTok{    x}
\NormalTok{\}}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ my\_max[T](a: T, b: T) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ T \{}
    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ a }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}=}\NormalTok{ b \{ a \} }\ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{ \{ b \}}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Generics in IRIS are \textbf{monomorphized} at compile time, generating
efficient concrete implementations for each type used.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{6.4 Trait Constraints
(\texttt{where})}{6.4 Trait Constraints (where)}}\label{trait-constraints-where}

You can constrain generic parameters using the \texttt{where} keyword,
enforcing that types must implement specific traits:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ print\_item[T }\KeywordTok{where}\NormalTok{ T: Printable](x: T) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \DataTypeTok{i64}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(x}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{to\_string());}
    \DecValTok{0}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{Try It Yourself}\label{try-it-yourself-5}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Define a trait \texttt{Area} with a method
  \texttt{area(self:\ Self)\ -\textgreater{}\ f64}.
\item
  Implement \texttt{Area} for
  \texttt{record\ Circle\ \{\ radius:\ f64\ \}} and
